This is our moment. Let’s seize it.

Sarah Longwell • PAID

Hey guys, it’s Sarah.

We’ve been waiting ten years for this; for voters to deliver a full and unequivocal rebuke of Donald Trump. After so many elections on a knife’s edge, this time, finally, the blue wave was indisputably real. And it was spectacular.

While we’re rightly celebrating, I want us all to keep one thing in mind—this blue wave didn’t just happen. People worked for it, fought for it, and voted for it.

So much of what we’ve built at THE BULWARK has been about working toward an outcome like this—not just explaining what’s happening in this country, but actually changing it.

Last night’s results affirmed something we’ve been preaching for years: The threat was real, but so was the opportunity to build a coalition strong enough to push back.

What we do with this opportunity matters. And we need to move now.

Join now

Turning this rebuke of MAGA into lasting change is the job ahead—which is why we’re not taking any days off. People are paying attention, looking for answers, and deciding where to turn next. The Democrats’ victory in the midterms wasn’t the end of the fight. It was the beginning.

That’s exactly what we’re doing at THE BULWARK. Fighting the good fight.

We’ll keep holding the powerful to account, telling you the truth, and growing this pro-democracy community. We’re going to be in the thick of it over these next two years. We’re going to be investing more in the reporting and analysis that have become our trademark. We’re going to be giving you the news—without fear or favor.

And we want you with us.

So we’re celebrating this moment with a special 20% FLASH SALE on Bulwark+. If you’ve been waiting for the right moment to join us, this is that moment.

FIGHT THE GOOD FIGHT

An authoritarian’s greatest weakness lies in his opponents’ refusal to back down, or go away, or be divided. That’s why THE BULWARK community exists: to lift each other up, energize and motivate one another, and remind the rest of the country that giving up is never an option. I cannot stress how rare this moment is. Or how important.

So, yes, we will celebrate the midterm results. And we will keep our eyes on the future. Because the next 24 months are going to be incredibly important for the health of our democracy. And they’re going to be hectic. And no one’s going to cover them like THE BULWARK.

THIS IS OUR MOMENT. LET’S MAKE SOMETHING OF IT. COME RIDE WITH US.

—Sarah

World Models Just Went Open-Source

Last week, Google's Genie 4 showed how far AI-generated worlds have come. This week, I found something that might matter even more.

I shared an open-source world model that does a lot of what Genie 4 does, and the big difference is you can actually try it right now:

Interactive environments generated on the fly, not just video clips you watch.

Open-source so developers can run it, study it, and build on top of it.

Available to test today, instead of sitting behind a research preview.

My POV: The closed labs still set the pace, but open-source keeps closing the gap faster than anyone expects. We saw it with language models, then image and video models, and now it's happening with world models. Once anyone can tinker with this stuff, the most interesting uses usually come from people nobody was paying attention to.

Hit reply and let me know: would you rather have the most polished model behind a waitlist, or a slightly rougher one you can use today?

— Matt

Anthropic Reveals Eye-Watering Numbers Ahead of IPO

Via CNBC

Anthropic disclosed new financial details in an updated S-1 filing ahead of its highly anticipated public debut, revealing enormous revenue growth alongside nearly $900 billion in infrastructure commitments.

By the numbers:

$17 billion in Q3 revenue, up from $1.4 billion a year earlier.

47% quarterly revenue growth, down from 144% in the previous quarter.

$23.3 billion net loss, narrowed from $66.3 billion in Q2.

$895.4 billion in purchase and lease commitments, almost entirely related to computing infrastructure, with approximately $69.8 billion due over the next 12 months.

The infrastructure gamble: Anthropic is committing extraordinary sums to the computing power required to train and run Claude. Its filing warns that some infrastructure partners rely on outside financing, meaning delays or higher costs could affect the capacity Anthropic has contracted for.

The bigger picture: These numbers are enormous, even by AI industry standards. Anthropic is generating revenue at an astonishing pace, but its spending commitments show just how expensive staying at the frontier has become. As the company approaches its stock market debut, investors will have to decide whether that growth can continue long enough to justify the valuation, or whether slowing momentum and mounting infrastructure costs will eventually catch up.

DeepSeek Takes Aim at Claude Code

Via Financial Post

Chinese AI startup DeepSeek released a prototype of its new open-source coding assistant, Super-Agent, designed to challenge Anthropic's Claude Code.

What it does: Unlike traditional coding assistants that wait for a prompt, Super-Agent can proactively monitor developers' group chats, identify coding problems, and suggest solutions. The idea is to make AI less of a tool developers constantly supervise and more of a teammate that notices when something needs attention.

A crowded field: Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, Cursor, and a growing list of coding agents are already competing for developers' attention. DeepSeek's open-source approach could appeal to teams seeking more control over their tools, although this is still a prototype, so its performance against established competitors remains to be seen.

The bigger picture: Coding has become one of the most competitive and commercially promising areas of AI. Nearly every major lab is racing to build assistants that don't just suggest code but actually complete development tasks. DeepSeek's entry shows how quickly the market is evolving, with the next battleground increasingly focused on autonomy, workflow integration, and how little developers need to intervene.

NEWSLETTER · 04 Nov 2026, 18:02

Conscious uncoupling

Tonight's edition also includes: Stalling Ukraine-Europe talks on alternatives to Starlink

VIEW IN BROWSER

By Victoria Becker

Supported by

Hello, and welcome to Rapporteur PM.

Ukraine, whose EU membership bid was once “coupled” with Moldova, has now slipped behind Chișinău.

The European Commission's latest enlargement assessment said that even if “Ukraine continues to accelerate the pace of reforms”, it can at best complete an “ambitious objective to close accession negotiations by the end of 2028”.

In contrast, the Commission said Moldova could meet its “achievable objective of closing accession negotiations by early 2028”, consciously uncoupling the two accession hopefuls in what is, at the end of the day, a merit-based process and not a buddy system.

Moldova joined enlargement talks with Ukraine in June 2022, a linkage that was politically symbolic to both countries, which see EU membership as critical to resisting Russian domination.

Breaking the coupling to allow Chișinău to overtake Kyiv is seen in Ukraine as a political signal calling into question European commitment to its membership.

Moldova has made "remarkable progress", said Marta Kos, the EU's enlargement chief, adding that the country was "the best performer in her class".

Ukraine's hopes, on the other hand, have been dampened by a full year at the least, the Commission estimates, though acknowledging extraordinary progress during wartime.

"Ukraine continues to advance under the most difficult circumstances," Kos said, but was unable to offer a date when asked about when Ukraine could become a member of the EU.

Kyiv is aiming to close several ‘chapters', policy areas, of negotiating reforms during the Lithuanian Council of the EU presidency in early 2027, two Ukrainian officials told Rapporteur earlier this week.

It would mark the first time Ukraine closed chapters on its path to becoming an EU country. However, they require conquering the high hurdle of the so-called ‘IBAR’, or Interim Benchmark Assessment Report.

"The joint task force we launched in October is poised to advance essential rule of law and anti-corruption reforms," Kos said, referring to the group established with Sergii Koretskyi, Ukraine's prime minister.

"It will help Ukraine advance towards the interim benchmark assessment report in the first half of 2027."

All of this, however, is conditional on the final two 'clusters' being opened by the end of the year – which requires all 27 EU capitals to be on board.

Supported by Cement Europe

One industry’s by-product, another’s raw material

Materials such as fly ash, slag and foundry sand already replace natural raw materials in cement. Less extraction, less landfill, less exposure to volatile markets. A local industry, providing solutions. Learn more here.

Round-up

SLASH, SLASH: The European Commission has threatened tougher legal action against countries that fail to implement its red tape-slashing agenda, as Brussels steps up efforts to ease regulatory burdens for business and integrate the EU’s fragmented single market.

DEAD TAX WALKING? The EU executive is keeping alive a 2018 digital tax proposal, according to its final work programme for 2027, despite plans to withdraw it in an earlier draft. Taxing tech giants is highly sensitive, given that many of them are American and the US and the EU are already at loggerheads over other tech rules.

CRISIS MANAGER: Carlos Cuerpo, Spain’s economy minister and deputy prime minister, will “temporarily” take over responsibility for railways following the transport minister’s unexpected resignation, Pedro Sánchez, the country’s leader, announced on Wednesday.

TECH DIFFICULTIES: The Commission informed EU countries on Tuesday that talks designed to help Ukraine get easier and cheaper access to European Starlink alternatives for satcoms are stalling, as Ukraine has not enough administrative capacity to engage with the EU executive, Rapporteur can exclusively reveal.

What Is Needed for Tokenization to Deliver

Tokenization, the representation of assets and liabilities on programmable distributed ledgers, has the potential to transform financial markets by streamlining the asset life cycle, reducing frictions, and improving transparency. Yet tokenized markets remain small and fragmented, and their growth depends on legal certainty, regulatory clarity, interoperability, and the availability of settlement assets. Policymakers should adopt a technology-neutral approach that removes unnecessary barriers while safeguarding financial stability.

Read the Blog

What Is Needed for Tokenization to Deliver

Read the Blog

Tokenization, the representation of assets and liabilities on programmable distributed ledgers, has the potential to transform financial markets by streamlining the asset life cycle, reducing frictions, and improving transparency. Yet tokenized markets remain small and fragmented, and their growth depends on legal certainty, regulatory clarity, interoperability, and the availability of settlement assets. Policymakers should adopt a technology-neutral approach that removes unnecessary barriers while safeguarding financial stability.

Too Big to Rig

The beginning of the end of MAGA?

William Kristol, Andrew Egger

READ TO ME

Not done basking in the warm sunshine of good news? Us neither. Tim, Sarah, and JVL will be live on Substack and YouTube at 10:00 a.m. EST, with (possibly) Sam and Bill to follow after. Happy, happy Wednesday.

(Photo illustration by Sarah Rogers/The Bulwark | Photos: Getty, Shutterstock)

Too Big to Rig

by Andrew Egger

The whole game changed last night. We’re all still grappling with what Democrats’ once-in-a-century rout will mean. JVL is right: Trumpism has not been fully defeated, not yet. But one of its darkest elements has been smashed for good.

In 2020, Trump tried to steal a presidential election by sheer force. After he failed, he and his movement spent years systematically grinding down the institutions that got in his way. After his re-election, he stocked the government with loyalists and attempted to seize unprecedented federal control over elections. The inescapable question has hung in the air like a miasma: What happens if he tries to do it again?

Last night, those fears popped like a soap bubble. Whatever dirty tricks Trump might have been planning—and you can bet he was planning them—were trampled by the stampede of the Democratic wave,¹ which was, to borrow a phrase, too big to rig.

For eight years, we’ve been building a pro-democracy community unlike any other. There’s never been a better time to join it.

Join

Yesterday morning, Trump was deploying a thousand DOJ employees to monitor polling places in blue areas and leading the charge for Alaska to refuse to certify a Democratic victory in the Senate race, while MAGA influencers got busy circulating election-fraud conspiracies and lies. Hours later, Trump was meekly promising to accept the results and work across the aisle. “We’ll, you know, look forward to working with [Democrats],” he told the New York Times. “We’ll probably work something out.”

Trump’s stop-the-steal plans didn’t just get trampled in 2026. They’re also DOA for 2028. Trying to steal an election requires a lot of institutional support that Trump simply won’t have in two years: No Republican Congress to vote not to certify races, fewer Republican governors in swing states to help him monkey with the totals where he’d need it. Six of the seven 2024 presidential swing states had governor’s elections last night; Democrats won all six.

Trump’s problems won’t end there. And neither will his party’s. Elected Republicans are now caught in an excruciating bind: They’ve arrived at Trumpism’s electoral dead end, but the combination of Trump’s massive, touchy ego and the total lack of other power centers in the party means they have zero cover to try to stake out new territory either.

For Republicans trying to figure out their political future, that means either continuing to tie themselves to the lame-duck president, or risking having him try to end their careers out of sheer pique. Meanwhile, the factional knives are coming out: Not many Republicans are brave enough to blame Trump for this loss, but you can bet that, for instance, supporters and opponents of the war in Iran will be spending the rest of the year trying to lay this disaster at each other’s feet.

But right now, in the afterglow of the Democrats’ world-historical stomp, I’m setting all that aside for the moment to focus on one simple, shining fact. Trump’s election lies—which have done unbelievable damage to America since 2020 and until yesterday seemed an indelible part of our political moment—now suddenly have been utterly neutralized, such that it no longer seems insane to contemplate the possibility that Trump will never successfully peddle that poison again.

A Great Day for the Good Guys

by William Kristol

Let it be told to the future world, that in the depth of winter, when nothing but hope and virtue could survive, that the city and the country, alarmed at one common danger, came forth to meet and to repulse it.

–Thomas Paine, The American Crisis No. 1, December 23, 1776

Election Day 2026 was the true celebration of our 250th anniversary.

The childish events staged earlier this year by Donald Trump, for Donald Trump, and about Donald Trump, are already fading into the mists of history. The votes of perhaps one hundred and twenty million Americans yesterday are what will be remembered about this year.

It was a heck of a night, a bigger wave than even those of us who were bullish on a wave anticipated.

Indeed, it’s probably time to retire the “wave” metaphor. I’ve used it many times, and it does capture the way in which an election like yesterday’s can sweep over barriers and sweep away obstacles. But it suggests that what happened was some kind of force of nature.

It was a human choice. Americans chose to repudiate a debased and degraded version of what this nation should be, and instead to open the door to a better future.

I dare say Thomas Paine would have been proud. So would Alexander Hamilton, who wrote in the first Federalist Paper that

It has been frequently remarked that it seems to have been reserved to the people of this country, by their conduct and example, to decide the important question, whether societies of men are really capable or not of establishing good government from reflection and choice, or whether they are forever destined to depend for their political constitutions on accident and force.

Yesterday was a notable act of reflection, and a striking instance of choice, on the part of the people of this country.

The question of course, as it was at the founding, is what comes next. In choosing to hand the Republican party an historic defeat, the public gave the Democratic party a landslide victory. Democrats seem to have won the national popular vote by as much as 14 points, the largest margin in any House election—midterm or presidential year—in half a century. Democrats picked up between seven and nine Senate seats and something on the order of forty-five house seats. Democrats will now hold a majority of the nation’s governorships, including those of thirteen of our fourteen largest states.

So the Democratic party has a mandate. But as yesterday’s election wasn’t a normal one, the party’s mandate isn’t a normal one. The mandate is to look beyond the normal horizons of party politics. It’s a challenge to Democrats to think big. And it’s an opportunity for Democrats to act not just as Democrats but as defenders and strengtheners of liberal democracy; not just as representatives reflecting the will of the people but as leaders entrusted with high office to help shape a better future for all of us.

Can the Democrats rise to this challenge and opportunity? This will be a major topic for discussion in the days, months, and years to come. But surely we’re entitled now to a day not of thinking or worrying or arguing, but of celebrating.

But only one day!

Join now

MAGA Drowns

In case you missed it, JVL wrote an emergency Triad late last night just as the full magnitude of the Democrats’ victory was coming into view.

(1) As big as we thought.

The polls slightly understated Democratic strength. Florida nearly flipped both the governor and Senate. Annie Andrews didn’t squeak out a win over Darline Graham—she thumped her. Same with James Talarico. Same with Adam Hamilton.

Gina Hinojosa, who ran a race almost entirely on her own, beat a three-term incumbent governor in TEXAS.

We don’t have the final numbers yet, but Susan Collins is almost gone and Michigan looks safe.

As I write this, 54 seats is the baseline. 55 seats is probable. 56 might materialize before the counting is done.

The double-nickel. Remember where you read that first.

(2) Size matters.

In the House, the next speaker will have a lot of room to maneuver. He can protect all of these new Democrats by letting them trade off on tough votes.

In the Senate, 55 seats means that impeachment of cabinet members is a live possibility. Could you get 12 Republicans to join in removing the architect of the Iran war?

I’d like to test that proposition.

Welcome back, Superhuman. What are you watching tonight? Unless you’re mid-show or watching an old favorite, you probably don’t know. It’s one of the most frustrating couch questions, but Netflix just proposed an AI-powered answer. Plus, an unlikely celebrity joined the AI startup game.

Today: Bigger models aren’t always better, how to create an impossible stunt video starring yourself with Seedance 2.5, and get the latest prompts and trending social posts.

TODAY IN AI

Watch how Netflix’s AI Discover feature helps you find new movies or shows. Source: Netflix.

1. Netflix tests AI-powered recommendations: Netflix introduced AI Discover, a new tab on its TV app that helps you find something to watch based on your mood. You start by picking an AI-generated theme like “Scare Me” or “Quick Watches,” then narrow down from there. It’s powered by a new intelligence layer that responds to feedback, and Netflix is treating it like a “test kitchen” for trying out new discovery features. It’s rolling out to a small group first, with wider access coming soon.

2. Steve Aoki’s content creation startup opens its waitlist: Selendis, an AI startup built by DJ Steve Aoki alongside industry experts, just introduced Archon, a video foundation model that turns raw footage into finished edits. The company claims Archon learns your personal editing style and powers an AI-native video editor that multiple people can use at once. It’s in early access now, with an open waitlist.

3. OpenAI plans a ChatGPT shake-up and teases a new model: According to emails OpenAI sent to users, the lab is preparing to merge ChatGPT’s Chat and Work tabs into one platform, a move that would mirror Anthropic’s merge of Claude and Cowork in September. Codex will stay separate, but it will share the same usage pool. OpenAI also added voice chat to its personal finance tool, while a cryptic post from product chief Thibault Sottiaux hints that a new model may be landing soon.

TUTORIAL

How to create an impossible stunt video starring yourself with Seedance 2.5

Photo: Seedance TV

Open Higgsfield > Video, then choose Seedance 2.5 as your model.

Upload 2–5 clear reference photos of yourself, plus an optional stunt clip for motion or camera inspiration.

Add your references to the generation and set the output to 9:16 if you’re making a Reel or TikTok.

Sample Prompt: “Create a cinematic impossible stunt starring the person in my reference images. Show me [stunt] in [location], using the reference clip for motion and camera inspiration. Preserve my face, hair, outfit, and proportions throughout, with realistic body movement, convincing scale, dynamic camera work, and natural lighting. Structure the scene as setup > stunt > reaction > payoff.”

Set the duration, generate a Draft first, and check the face consistency, stunt motion, framing, and pacing.

Regenerate the strongest version at higher quality, or open Seedance 2.5 Edit to fix specific sections where the face, motion, or camera drifts.

FROM THE FRONTIER

Bigger isn’t always better. Using top models could be costing you more money — and getting worse results

Made with Midjourney

The AI industry is obsessed with leaderboards, with many labs tuning their models to ace tests. But a high ranking doesn’t always mean a model will excel at your specific tasks. The same goes for a model’s reasoning effort.

New research found that higher reasoning effort often fails on tasks that lower settings can solve, while costing up to 42x more for straightforward tasks. So instead of defaulting to frontier models at max reasoning, many leaders are fine-tuning smaller models to fit their needs:

Fermisense spent $500 to train a custom 9B open model that beat all five leading frontier models while running 68x cheaper.

Nvidia and CrowdStrike built a defensive cybersecurity system that outscored top models at a 99% lower cost.

Palantir said that a fine-tuned Nvidia model outperformed frontier models on real customer tasks.

The same applies to benchmarks. Instead of relying on third-party leaderboards, many companies are building internal benchmarks to grade new models on tasks they actually care about. You can build a similar model evaluation system:

Pick a few tasks critical to your day-to-day, where you know what great work looks like.

When a new model drops, test it out on your tasks.

Compare the outputs and switch only if it beats your current model.

That way, you’ll know a new model is worth it before jumping over.

Top NK stories from around the web

What happens if North Korea steals Wall Street's tokenized assets next CoinDesk

Hackers managed to pull off the biggest crypto heist ever last week, stealing about $6.3 billion from Binance, the world's largest crypto exchange, in mere minutes. Binance said about $3 billion of the affected assets had since been frozen and pledged to cover the losses with its own funds. Both U.S. law enforcement, including the Federal Bureau of Investigation, and a slew of blockchain intelligence firms and the FBI have attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking unit.

Navy stages maritime maneuver drills to hone operability Korea Times

The Navy has launched maritime maneuver drills in waters off the country's eastern, western and southern coasts in a bid to hone its operational capabilities against various maritime threats, the armed service said Wednesday. The Republic of Korea Navy Task Fleet Command is taking part in the drills, which kicked off Sunday for a six-day run, involving nearly a dozen warships and other aircraft, according to the Navy.

Modernizing North Korea’s 240 mm MLRS: Precision, Automation, and Extended Range 38 North

North Korea conducted a test firing of an upgraded 240 mm multiple-launch rocket system (MLRS) on September 22, according to a September 24 report by the Korean Central News Agency (KCNA). The test is believed to have taken place at the headquarters of the 425th Mechanized Infantry Division in Sukchon County, South Pyongan Province, with the rockets reportedly traveling approximately 50–60 kilometers toward a target island. KCNA described the system as incorporating an autonomous precision-guidance system and claimed that the rockets could reach 100 kilometers in ballistic-flight mode and 115 kilometers using a combined glide-flight mode.

Kim Yo-jong's rare denial of $6.3b crypto heist raises questions over US-N. Korea dialogue Korea Herald

Experts say the unusually swift and high-profile nature of the statement from Kim — a department director of the Workers' Party of Korea's Central Committee and North Korean leader Kim Jong-un's sister — may reflect Pyongyang's concerns for its international reputation and an attempt to prevent the issue from complicating potential diplomatic engagement with Washington.

Senior officials of S. Korea, Switzerland discuss N. Korea situation after Swiss FM's Pyongyang trip Yonhap News

Senior officials of South Korea and Switzerland have discussed bilateral cooperation and the situation on the Korean Peninsula following the Swiss foreign minister's recent visit to North Korea, Seoul's foreign ministry said Wednesday. Deputy Foreign Minister for Political Affairs Chung Eui-hae held the seventh round of bilateral policy consultations with Markus Leitner, Switzerland's assistant state secretary, in Seoul on Tuesday and exchanged views on major regional issues, including the situation on the Korean Peninsula and in Northeast Asia, according to the ministry.

OpenAI added 200 million users over just the past two months. Its growth is accelerating. This post contains an edited transcript of an interview with ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry, where he discusses OpenAI's plans to merge ChatGPT's Chat and Work modes, image generation, interactive interfaces, advertising, education, and public unease about AI.

Developers can now enable their apps to bring content and actions directly into Siri AI by using App Intents. Skills are available for more complex requests that require Siri to combine several actions into a single experience. Siri AI can now surface any entity in the Spotlight semantic index. Submissions to the App Store for apps with the new capabilities will open up in the spring.

Science & Futuristic Technology

Neuralink's China Rival Targets Obesity With ‘Electronic' GLP-1 (2 minute read)

BrainCo, a Chinese neurotech company, is developing a wearable device that suppresses appetite by stimulating the brain. It aims to bring the device to market within the next two to three years. The device will launch first in China before potentially expanding to the US. BrainCo currently markets brain-computer interface-enabled headsets to address neurological disorders such as autism, ADHD, and insomnia. Its devices can collect, decode, and stimulate neural signals.

The State of Longevity (18 minute read)

The fringe movement of biohackers and people taking a more hands-on approach to longevity has moved into the mainstream thanks to the COVID-19 pandemic and AI chatbots. More and more people are now taking their health into their own hands and spending time, money, and energy on health and longevity. This post looks at how people view health and explores how the people at the cutting edge of health and technology approach their own health. Some of the biggest opportunities in longevity will go to the companies that help people take action and manage their health over time.

Programming, Design & Data Science

Meeting notes that get the details right (Sponsor)

Wispr Flow Notetaker uses your dictionary and calendar to catch uncommon names and industry jargon, then pulls out decisions and next steps. Ask anything across your meetings and get linked to the source. Free on Mac and Windows. Try Notetaker free

How software engineering is changing: a tale of two eras (32 minute read)

AI-native startups can ship even faster than fast-moving pre-AI startups because they risk nothing by moving too fast. The old constraints of software engineering have disappeared, allowing startups to move faster, but also break things. The new constraints are too much context switching, losing shared context, tougher pacing, less focus on design, and AI plan limits. Many existing technologies and processes will crack under the change.

Interpolative Software (5 minute read)

Interpolative software is code that is solidly within the training distribution of models. It can often be specified with just a few sentences of prompting. Novel software is something that has fundamentally not existed before. Writing novel software with agents is challenging, as they are not trained on the task. Not a lot of software is novel, which is fine, as there is still a ton of value in the interpolative stuff.

Miscellaneous

Google Will Turn Off Search for Schools That Reject AI (7 minute read)

Google will turn off AI tools in its software for K-12 students unless school administrators explicitly choose to make them available. However, schools that choose to restrict AI entirely will also lose access to Google search. Google had earlier turned on AI features as the default option and is claiming that the reversal is in response to feedback from educators and parents. The company's decision to use this strategy shows how much power it has over the US education system.

The Death of the Angel Investor (6 minute read)

The economics of angel investing have shifted. Many investors have realized it's potentially more profitable to raise a fund instead of using their own money. Technology startups now move much faster, and running around grabbing small checks from angels is less palatable than grabbing a pre-seed fund to fund a whole initial round. Also, when angels try to fund a round, they are often pushed out by the valuation game.

Michael Jinghan Zeng: When Confucius Institutes Become a Symbol of Great-Power Competition

Former Confucius Institute director questions whether language and cultural programmes can deliver the political identification their Chinese funders may hope for.

Michael Jinghan Zeng: When Confucius Institutes Become a Symbol of Great-Power Competition

Former Confucius Institute director questions whether language and cultural programmes can deliver the political identification their Chinese funders may hope for.

Yuxuan JIA and Yiyang Xu

Nov 4

READ IN APP

On 23 October 2026, as the Pentagon designated Confucius Institutes as a national security threat, US Secretary of War Pete Hegseth claimed that 80 “new propaganda cells” had emerged under different names at 30 US universities, accusing them of stealing intellectual property and seeking to “infiltrate the minds of American students”.

Michael Jinghan Zeng, Professor and Head of the Department of Public and International Affairs at City University of Hong Kong, draws on his own experience in responding to Hegseth’s remarks. From 2019 to 2025, Zeng served as Foreign Director of Lancaster University’s Confucius Institute, where he led a team of nearly 30 staff.

Below is the article Zeng published in FT Chinese, the Chinese-language service of the Financial Times, on 30 October.

The East is Read and Pekingnology have already carried many excerpts from Zeng’s books, his articles, and podcast conversations. He is the author of the three-volume Memoirs of a Confucius Institute Director, the first two volumes of which were already published by Palgrave Macmillan in 2025 and 2026. The Chinese edition of the first volume, Controversies and Realities of Confucius Institutes, was published by City University of Hong Kong Press in 2026. The third volume is forthcoming.

当孔子学院成为大国竞争的符号

When Confucius Institutes Become a Symbol of Great-Power Competition

At the end of 2022, I started a weekend Chinese class at Lancaster University’s Confucius Institute. The Chinese classes previously available in the area had been suspended due to the pandemic, and many parents wanted their children to continue learning. The university’s facilities and teaching staff made it possible to reorganise the classes. The initiative grew out of a very practical need among local families.

That experience stands in sharp contrast to the image of Confucius Institutes in international politics. On 23 October, US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth claimed that “80 new propaganda cells across 30 universities had popped up using different names and different charters”, and that one of their missions was “to steal intellectual property”. He also announced that the Pentagon had added them to its list of institutions that threaten US national security.

My experience in Britain gives me no position to assess the specific circumstances of these US institutions. But Hegseth’s statement has made me think: why has a cooperative platform for language and cultural education come to embody such grand ideas about national power? In these debates, the needs of local communities are often quickly lost from view.

I served as the foreign director of the Confucius Institute at Lancaster University from 2019 to 2025. Over those six years, I increasingly came to see that the two opposing views of Confucius Institutes might share a common assumption: that an institution’s presence overseas is simply an extension of Chinese state power.

Seen this way, supporters can point to the number of institutes and the scale of their activities as evidence of China’s growing influence. Critics, looking at the same map, can see them as outposts for infiltrating Chinese influence. The two sides reach very different conclusions, but both may overestimate a state’s ability to reshape another society simply by establishing organisations there. The crucial question is whether cultural contact can produce the kind of political identification that the funder hopes to foster.

I wrote a three-volume memoir of my time as a Confucius Institute director partly to examine this process. The first volume, Challenges, Controversies, and Realities, draws on my experience as a director to recount the disruption caused by the pandemic, the dissolution of the Confucius Institute Headquarters (Hanban), and the wave of Confucius Institute closures in Britain. The second volume examines how local realities shape overseas projects. The third, as-yet-unpublished volume turns to the Chinese side, exploring the different roles played by Chinese partner universities, Hanban, and the Chinese International Education Foundation in the development and operation of Confucius Institutes. Taken together, the three volumes show that cooperation often takes a different course from the one originally envisaged and has to be continually negotiated.

The changes at the headquarters are particularly telling. In 2020, the dissolution of Hanban shifted how the institutes were funded and governed. In the first volume, I describe in detail how sudden this organisational change was and the uncertainty it created for institutes on the ground. Yet overseas institutes did not disappear with the headquarters. Existing university partnerships and local demand allowed them to continue operating.

This change does not prove that China has withdrawn from the Confucius Institute programme, nor does it in itself dispel concerns about Chinese funding. It does, however, show why any assessment of an overseas project must take account of changes in its institutional relationships. If every change in structure is treated as a smokescreen for the same strategy, it becomes difficult to examine the institutional changes on their own terms.

Local needs can also shape what a project means. In Blackpool, the Confucius Institute worked with local primary schools to teach Chinese. The UK Office for Standards in Education, Children’s Services and Skills (Ofsted)’s 2019 inspection report on one of these schools looked at whether pupils were learning about the world “beyond Blackpool” and noted that Year 6 pupils could already hold short conversations in Mandarin. For the school, the value of the classes lay in giving children the opportunity to encounter a society beyond their own experience.

Such educational benefits, of course, cannot excuse any wrongdoing. But treating them all as evidence of growing Chinese state influence overlooks the aims of those on the receiving end. A British school may actively use resources provided by China simply because they serve its pupils’ interests. Likewise, a parent’s decision to have a child learn Chinese does not necessarily imply any position on China’s political system.

The same point applies to China’s own cultural exchange efforts. It is easy to count how many institutions receive funding and how many activities are organised, but these are measures of inputs and outputs, not of what participants actually think of China. An institute may be able to run language classes, but that does not mean it can determine what its audience thinks about China beyond the classroom.

Students who learn Chinese may come away with a more complex understanding of China, or even a more critical one. They may appreciate Chinese literature while questioning Chinese policies. Being open to such outcomes is part of taking education seriously. If success in cultural exchange is defined in advance by whether foreign audiences come to identify more strongly with China, institutions on the ground are being asked to deliver a political outcome that is difficult to achieve and beyond the remit of language education.

Security assessments must also allow for this distinction. A person’s willingness to engage with Chinese culture should not be taken as evidence that they share Beijing’s political position. Nor does China’s funding of a project mean that those involved have no aims of their own. Hegseth’s serious allegations should be tested against the actual conduct of the organisations concerned. Whether political motives are involved in cultural cooperation is one question; whether those motives can be realised as intended is another.

Once these two questions are conflated, cultural exchange itself comes under strain. Funders seek to show that their projects are expanding national influence, while critics treat any sign of such influence as a threat. Institutions on the ground are then left to explain why they are still needed. The more effort they spend managing these political expectations, the less attention they can give to teaching and working with local communities.

A more useful way to think about China’s overseas cultural cooperation is to ask how local partners can have a genuine say in what these projects are for. Can schools adapt courses to their students’ needs? Can local communities set goals that funders had not anticipated? Can participants continue to hold their own views of China? All of these should be taken into account when assessing the success of such cooperation.

This also means that the Chinese side must be prepared to accept that success may take a less visible form: a project may make a genuine contribution to local education without translating into political identification with China. Those receiving funding may remain willing to cooperate while retaining their critical views and keeping some distance. Such relationships may prove more durable than a high-profile publicity campaign, even if their value is difficult to capture in any straightforward measure of national influence.

Hegseth’s latest statement has once again put Confucius Institutes in the spotlight of great-power competition. But in those weekend classrooms in Lancaster, the immediate questions were much more down-to-earth: what kind of classes would suit the children, whether families could afford them, and whether teachers could teach them well. The future of international cooperation ultimately depends on how well it addresses practical questions like these.

Is India Extending Its Control Over Islamic Practice In Kashmir?

Plus: Thailand's 'bamboo diplomacy' and China's fifth plenum.‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­‌ ­

November 4, 2026 thediplomat.com

Welcome to the latest issue of Diplomat Brief. This week we examine Indian government efforts to exert control over Islamic practices in Kashmir, dig into the pitfalls of Thailand’s “bamboo diplomacy” and check in on China’s fifth plenum.

STORY OF THE WEEK

FEATURE

Is India Extending Its Control Over Islamic Practice In Kashmir?

Ghulam Sofi’s forehead bears a dark mark right in the center; it is from years of offering namaz, he says. It is Friday and Sofi is getting ready for the prayers at his home in the Khanyar area of Indian-administered Kashmir’s Srinagar city.

As Sofi neatly adjusts his skullcap, without ruining the thin layer of grey hair under it, he says, “In Kashmir, Islam and politics have always remained inseparable. We can smell the link this time too.”

Sofi is referring to the recent order by Kashmir’s Waqf Board – a body overseeing Muslim shrines in the region and headed by a Kashmiri leader associated with Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s Hindu supremacist Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) – which proposed developing common themes for Friday sermons across mosques and shrines in the conflicted region.

Locals feel that the board’s plan to dictate Friday sermons is an assault on their faith, dressed up as “coordination.”

Find out more

Videos

VIDEO

Why China Can’t Fix Its Broken Economy

Our YouTube channel, The Diplomat explains major topics and trends in the Asia-Pacific in video form. Our latest video dives deep into a serious problem: China's economy is fundamentally broken. Chinese people just aren’t spending enough money and this is not just a problem for China, it’s a problem for the world.

In “Our Hearts Burned for Home: Uyghur Militants in Syria’s Civil War” journalist Emily Feng explores how a few thousand Uyghurs from Xinjiang ended up on the front lines of Syria’s civil war.

When you buy any book through our Bookshop.org shop, a portion of the proceeds comes back to The Diplomat.

Find out more

Behind the News

INTERVIEW

The Pitfalls of Thailand’s ‘Bamboo Diplomacy’

Thailand has long prided itself on its foreign policy acumen and its status as the only nation in Southeast Asia that avoided colonization by the West. At the center of this is the notion of “bamboo diplomacy” – a form of diplomacy that prioritizes pragmatism and flexibility in pursuit of the national interest.

But as Pongkwan Sawasdipakdi, a lecturer in international relations at Thammasat University in Bangkok, told The Diplomat’s Sebastian Strangio, “I would hesitate to characterize bamboo diplomacy as a coherent doctrine. It is better understood as a broad diplomatic philosophy that prioritizes adaptability and survival rather than a clearly defined set of foreign policy principles.”

Find out more

This Week in Asia

NORTHEAST ASIA

China’s Fifth Plenum Pledges to Clean up the CCP – Again

From October 26-29, the Chinese Communist Party held the fifth plenary session of its 20th Central Committee – the fifth plenum, for short. The meeting focused on CCP “self-governance,” a favorite theme of CCP General Secretary Xi Jinping. Xi has made anti-corruption a hallmark of his leadership since taking office in 2012, but 14 years on and the purges are escalating, not slowing down. Corruption remains as big an issue as ever – no surprise, because the CCP insists on policing itself. Meanwhile, the heavy emphasis on extolling Xi’s virtues in the fifth plenum communique signaled that he will seek an unprecedented fourth consecutive term in office at the 21st Party Congress next year – and that, many analysts say, is the real motive behind the stepped-up corruption probes.

Find out more

SOUTH ASIA

New Faultlines as Violence in Manipur Spirals

Meitei organizations in the Indian northeastern state of Manipur held a massive rally in Imphal on October 31 demanding protection of indigenous people, abrogation of the Suspension of Operations (SoO) agreement with militant groups, and the resignation of Chief Minister Yumnam Khemchand Singh for failing to halt the ethnic strife. The rally, which was endorsed by the Naga apex body, the United Naga Council, came on the heels of a series of incidents over the past several months in the state. There has been a further escalation of the violence that began more than three years ago in May 2023 between Manipur’s Meitei majority and Kuki-Zo communities. So far, more than 300 people have been killed, and 49 are missing.

Find out more

SOUTHEAST ASIA

Indonesia Urges Russia to Stop Recruiting Citizens

Indonesia this week officially urged Russia to stop recruiting Indonesian citizens into its armed forces, following a report that an Indonesian national was killed while fighting in Ukraine. The move came after Ukraine’s Defense Intelligence Directorate (HUR) reported in late October that an Indonesian identified by the initials MT had been killed on the frontline in Ukraine. He was just one a growing number of Indonesians who have been found fighting with the Russian military, some of whom have been attracted to Russia by the prospect of civilian employment, only to find themselves tricked or forced into joining the armed forces. Foreign Ministry spokesperson Yvonne Mewengkang told the media that Jakarta had “asked the Russian government to take the necessary steps to ensure that Indonesian citizens are not recruited into the Russian military or other military-related formations.”

Find out more

CENTRAL ASIA

Should the Organization of Turkic States Expand to Include… Russia?

The 13th summit of the Organization of Turkic States (OTS) was hosted by Recep Tayyip Erdogan in Ankara on October 29-30. At the summit, Kazakh President Kassym-Jomart Tokayev proposed inviting Russia into the organization through the new OTS Plus mechanism or as an observer. Hungary became an observer in 2018, Turkmenistan in 2021, and the Turkish Republic of Northern Cyprus – a de facto state only recognized by Turkiye – in 2022, but allowing Russia to join in the same capacity would represent a new kind of expansion. OTS Plus, introduced in 2025 and not yet put into operation, allows the organization to engage with non-member states on a project-based approach. That may be a better fit for Moscow but the OTS needs to consider whether inviting Russia might restrain their choices.

Find out more

The Danger of Being a Female Livestreamer in China

NEWSLETTER · 04 Nov 2026, 12:15

What Was At Stake

Not just control of Congress

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

What Was At Stake

Not just control of Congress

Anne Applebaum

Nov 4

READ IN APP

Gas prices, the war in Iran, data centers, health care. Americans have debated all of these subjects for weeks. But something more fundamental than control of Congress was on the ballot in these midterm elections. Americans were also voting to decide whether the United States, 250 years after its founding, would remain a democracy.

President Trump, who tried to overturn the 2020 election, did his best to manipulate this one too. Never before has the federal government demanded voter lists from states, and lost so many court cases in the process. Gerrymandering is an old tradition, but it has never before been directed from the White House. This put real pressure on state Republican leaders to redraw boundaries, in some cases with the deliberate aim of eliminating Black members of Congress. Once, the federal government helped state officials keep elections safe. This year it was the states that had to mount a defense against Washington to protect their constitutional right to run elections.

By now, most of us recognize Trump’s assault on elections as part of a pattern. Since he started his second term, the president has closely followed a playbook originally built by autocratic leaders in places like Hungary and Russia. He began with a full-scale attack on civil servants, universities, the legal profession and scientists.

He appointed a conspiracy-theorist to dismantle America’s public health system and a narcissistic television host to run the most powerful army in the world. He used regulatory institutions to put pressure on journalists. He turned ICE into a federal paramilitary force. He let Elon Musk cut food aid and medical care for the world’s poor so abruptly and so incompetently that he wasted hundreds of millions of dollars and killed untold numbers of people in the process.

The White House oversaw assaults on America’s foreign broadcasters, cyber defense, counter-intelligence. The president used the power of his office to earn money for himself, his family and his business friends. He undermined America’s alliances, started a war in Iran and prolonged the Russian invasion of Ukraine through diplomatic incompetence and possibly greed.

Through this period of chaos and destruction, the Republican majority in Congress either cheered or remained silent. Instead of asserting the legislative branch’s right to check the president, control the budget, and investigate corruption, Congressional leaders let Trump break laws and norms egregiously and continuously. They let the president not only impose illegal tariffs, and therefore raise prices for all Americans, but they also looked the other way when he and his entourage bet money on the stock market while doing so. They refused to block his reckless war in Iran, they refused to restrain ICE even after it killed Americans and they refused to ask why Musk was given power over agencies that regulate his companies, or why taxes on the wealthiest Americans were cut even as poor Americans lost their healthcare benefits.

The next Congress can change this trajectory. Newly elected leaders must not only investigate the president’s worst abuses of power and help Americans recover from his destructive policies, but also restore the health of the political system. We may need to rethink the role of institutions that the president has weakened, or write laws that stop people with deep conflicts of interest from making American foreign policy. We have to confront an AI industry that is unable to control its models, and a healthcare system weakened by the president’s reckless policies.

Above all we need to focus on the deepest source of imbalance in our politics: No other democracy spends billions of dollars on election campaigns, or gives so many opportunities for the wealthiest citizens to try to shape the result. The Democrats now have a unique opportunity to redress this imbalance. It will take more than two years to build a government that serves the needs of Americans, not a small class of oligarchs, but they can begin now.

Of course this election isn’t over until all the results are certified and all of the new members of Congress are sworn in. And we should expect that the president, as in 2020, will be considering many different ways of undermining this result. Over the next few days, watch for lawsuits, or unexplained delays.

But Americans can also be cheered by the fact that despite everything, people all over the country voted today, as is their right. There were no meaningful attempts to stop the vote. Turnout in many places was significant. Over the past eighteen months, I’ve heard many people wonder out loud whether ordinary citizens, playing by the rules, could fight back against an autocratic president and shape their country’s future. democracy.

And it turns out, they can.

Thank you to everyone who voted!

NEWSLETTER · 04 Nov 2026, 12:07

📱 Apple cracks the door open to Siri AI

PLUS: Turn one product photo into three studio shots ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

Read online

Read Online | Sign Up | Advertise

Good morning, AI enthusiasts, and welcome to our 4,926 new readers. Years of Apple Intelligence and one full Siri rebuild later, the iPhone still doesn’t feel any more like a device built for the AI era. Could letting other apps in finally change that?

Apple just opened up building on its Siri AI to all developers, something the notoriously closed-off ecosystem rarely allows. But none of it reaches the App Store until spring, which could be a whole different AI era for a product already feeling generations behind.

Founders and solo operators: You’re doing the work of a whole team by yourself — show us how AI makes that possible. Outreach, invoicing, content, customer replies, anything counts. Submit your workflow for a chance to be featured in the newsletter.

In today’s AI rundown:

Apple lets any app plug into Siri AI

Google won’t give schools Search without AI

Turn one product photo into three studio shots

CrowdStrike’s ‘kill switch’ to stop rogue AI agents

LATEST DEVELOPMENTS

APPLE

🍎 Apple lets any app plug into Siri AI

Image source: Apple

The Rundown: Apple just released a developer preview of Siri AI in its latest software betas, giving apps of any kind a way to build their own features into the assistant — though developers can only test it for now, and customers are still months away.

The details:

Siri AI is Apple’s rebuilt assistant, able to see a user’s screen and read messages, and has been a waitlisted beta since iOS 27 shipped in September.

Developers can now add their own actions instead of just Apple’s initial preset list of options, and teach Siri multi-step jobs through new “skills.”

For now, app versions stay with a developer’s internal testers, App Store submissions start in spring, and Apple says rules for developers will come later.

The betas also bring China its first Apple Intelligence tools (minus Siri AI) and test new Apple Watch tools that listen in the background to transcribe nearby talk.

Why it matters: Apple letting any app build into Siri is a real crack in its walled garden, which is a big deal. Still, it leaves the work on developers, and between Siri AI’s confusing rollout and the progress other frontier labs have made in areas like computer use, the best AI features still feel a long way off for the world’s most popular phone.

TOGETHER WITH WIZ

🔒 Securing AI Agents 101

The Rundown: AI agents are rapidly emerging across enterprise environments: powering automation, chaining tools, and acting across systems. Securing AI Agents 101 is a one-page resource to help teams build a clear understanding of what AI agents are, how they operate, and where key security considerations show up.

Inside, you’ll find:

What makes an AI agent different from traditional tools

Top risks to watch, from shadow AI to excessive permissions

Four key questions to assess agent usage and exposure

Get the one-page guide to AI agents.

GOOGLE & AI IN SCHOOLS

🏫 Google won’t give schools Search without AI

Image source: Google

The Rundown: Google announced that K-12 student accounts will start with Search, Gemini, and Gemini Notebook all off early next year, potentially forcing districts that want AI out of classrooms to also give up Google Search for their students.

The details:

Education VP Chris Phillips said to The New York Times that “Search and A.I. are deeply integrated,” and students at AI-free schools will see a notice that it’s off.

NYC and LA, the two largest U.S. districts, restricted AI for ~1M students this semester, and Fairfax County, VA, built a plugin to hide Google’s AI summaries.

Google’s announcement also comes one day after the Nov. 2 deadline senators gave the company to answer questions about pushing AI into schools.

Ex-FTC Chair Lina Khan said Google is using “its search monopoly to coerce schools” and will “punish those who don’t fall in line.”

Google later defended the move on X as “more choice, not less,” but its post only describes Search with AI Mode and AI Overviews, never without them.

Why it matters: Google is essentially saying out loud what the trend has shown — Google Search is AI search now — and schools aren’t getting the old version back. The stranger situation will be AI-free districts with kids on Chromebooks, turning in work through Classroom, and writing in Docs, yet unable to use Search.

AI TRAINING

📷 Turn one product photo into three studio shots

The Rundown: In this guide, you’ll build a product-shoot app in Runway using an image of a vintage Polaroid camera. You’ll create coral, sunlit, and blue-lit scenes, then save the app so you can repeat the process.

Step-by-step:

Save the guide’s Polaroid photo and its credit. Open Runway App Studio and check that your plan and credit balance support a build

Paste the build prompt to create an app with three shoot presets. Check the product upload, scene menu, and download controls before running it

Upload the original photo and list the camera details to preserve. Make one coral studio shot and inspect the lens, rainbow stripe, buttons, and lettering

Run the same photo through the sunlit and blue-lit presets. Keep the camera’s shape and viewpoint consistent, and save each approved result

Publish the app for yourself, then open all three downloads. Keep the source credit and a record of any defects with the set

Pro tip: Use the original product photo for every scene. Starting from a flawed AI image can carry its mistakes into the next shot. Visit The Rundown University to get the full guide, materials, files, and more.

PRESENTED BY PITCH

📣 Teams riding the AI wave present with Pitch

The Rundown: The teams building AI make their slides with Pitch. It’s how Perplexity, fal, Legora, Poolside, and Synthesia create and deliver presentations at scale.

Pitch is an agentic presentation platform where you can:

Generate fully editable decks from your own templates, so every slide stays on brand

Connect via MCP to personalize slides with data from your CRM or call notes

Nurture deals with custom pitch rooms, and track how buyers engage with built-in analytics

Try Pitch.

CROWDSTRIKE & AI SECURITY

🛑 CrowdStrike’s ‘kill switch’ to stop rogue AI agents

Image source: CrowdStrike

The Rundown: Cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike unveiled the AI Kill Switch, adding an emergency stop to its Falcon Guardian security platform for businesses worried about their AI agents going rogue.

The details:

The switch targets an agent’s link to its AI model, not the model itself, while Falcon Guardian separately monitors tools like Claude Code and Codex.

A human admin has to pull the trigger, and every use gets logged, while shutting agents down directly on work devices and in the cloud is “coming soon.”

CEO George Kurtz explained it like a race car’s brake, saying it sits outside the AI so an agent “can’t argue with it, rewrite it, or wait it out.”

CrowdStrike is also buying monitoring firm Fiddler AI and security tester Dreadnode for a reported $495M, and added Claude Cowork protection in beta.

Why it matters: Politicians have spent the summer fighting over an AI kill switch, with a pair of bills and a California executive order aimed at rogue AI agents. CrowdStrike is bringing the same concept to enterprises… where the likelier scenario isn’t a frontier model escaping a lab, but an agent holding the company’s logins going off script.

QUICK HITS

COMMUNITY AI WORKFLOW OF THE DAY

▸ Erin built a self-drafting timesheet with Claude

Today’s workflow comes from reader Erin Reid:

“Late timesheets delayed accurate client billing for our advertising and marketing agency, so I built a Claude project that reconstructs my week from the tools I already use. It drafts time entries that I review and approve before anything reaches our system. It’s useful for anyone who logs time after the fact and loses track of smaller tasks.

It searches the emails I sent or replied to, my calendar meetings, files I edited in Google Drive, Slack threads I joined, work-related browsing, and Claude work sessions. It builds a daily timeline and groups related items into one block. An email, a doc edit, and a Slack thread about the same issue count once.

The project instructions require Claude never to invent work or pad hours, never to double-count, and to disclose any source it couldn’t search. Instead of trying to remember my week, I now review entries that already have project codes and descriptions, and it also surfaces work I had forgotten.”

See the workflow on our Community Workflow Hub →

Major Developments

-Democrats retook control of both chambers of Congress following yesterday’s elections, scoring major victories across the country.

In the House of Representatives, the latest projections show the party slated to hold 255 seats after all votes are tallied. Going into the election, Republicans held 218 seats to Democrats’ 214.

In the Senate, Democrats swept to the majority, winning nearly every competitive state election. The latest projections show the party slated to hold 55 seats after all votes are tallied, with major upset victories in South Carolina and Kansas.

In a victory speech, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, who is slated to be the next Speaker of the House, said: “This is not a partisan moment, this is a patriotic one. This is not a blue moment or a red moment, it’s a red, white, and blue moment because America has won tonight.”

View his full remarks here:

House Speaker Mike Johnson addressed supporters last night in Louisiana, where he said, “Republicans have much more work to do to give every American a shot at a better life, to ensure that the United States remains the freest, most prosperous, most powerful nation in the history of the world.”

He added: “We will never give up on that endeavor and that vision. A new Congress does not change that at all.”

He went on to warn: “I am not going to exaggerate when I tell you that there’s not a single American, not a single American family anywhere, that’s going to be made better off if Democrats turn Congress into a circus.”

View his full remarks here:

In a post to his social media just before 11:00 PM EST, President Donald Trump said: “I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!”

Meanwhile, in an interview with The New York Times, Trump said that he would accept the results of the elections, adding: “We’ll work with the Democrats, and we’ll, you know, look forward to working with them. We’ll probably work something out.”

As results came in last night, a White House official told CBS’ Weijia Jiang, “President Trump was not on the ballot, and it’s proven that Trump voters show up when he is on the ballot.”

-New Hampshire Republican Governor Kelly Ayotte secured a second term, defeating Democrat Cinde Warmington.

Today’s History

On this day in 2008, Barack Obama was elected the 44th president of the United States, becoming the first African American elected to the office.

In his remarks in Chicago that night, Obama said: “If there is anyone out there who still doubts that America is a place where all things are possible; who still wonders if the dream of our founders is alive in our time; who still questions the power of our democracy, tonight is your answer.”

In his concession speech, Republican John McCain congratulated Obama and noted the significance of his election, saying: “This is an historic election, and I recognize the special significance it has for African-Americans and for the special pride that must be theirs tonight.”

Obama secured a decisive victory over McCain, winning 365 electoral votes and 52.9% of the popular vote.

In 1980, Ronald Reagan was elected the 40th president of the United States, defeating incumbent President Jimmy Carter.

In his victory speech that night in Los Angeles, Reagan said: “I am not frightened by what lies ahead and I don't believe the American people are frightened by what lies ahead. Together, we're going to do what has to be done. We're going to put America back to work again.”

In his concession speech, Carter said: “I promised you four years ago that I would never lie to you. So, I can’t stand here tonight and say it doesn’t hurt.”

View his remarks here.

Reagan secured a decisive victory over Carter, winning 489 electoral votes and 50.7% of the popular vote.

In 1952, Dwight Eisenhower was elected the 34th president of the United States, defeating Democrat Adlai Stevenson in a landslide.

In his victory speech, Eisenhower praised Stevenson, reading a telegram that he sent to the Illinoisan in response to his congratulatory message.

Eisenhower’s message read: “It is clearly necessary that men and women of good will of both parties forget the political strife through which we have passed.”

Eisenhower secured a decisive victory over Stevenson, winning 442 electoral votes and 55.2% of the popular vote.

A Democratic Wave. A Republican Reckoning. What Comes Next?

Watch now (13 mins) | Q&A with Reed Galen on the scale of the blue wave, why voters are rejecting Republican candidates, and what’s next for the Democratic Party — and the country.
